The fractional powers of the sub-Laplacian in Carnot groups through an analytic continuation Thanks: F.C. and F.F. are partially supported by INdAM-GNAMPA 2023 project CUP E53C22001930001: Equazioni completamente non lineari locali e non locali and INdAM-GNAMPA 2024 project CUP E53C23001670001: Free boundary problems in noncommutative structures and degenerate operators . F.F. is partially funded by PRIN 2022 7HX33Z - CUP J53D23003610006, Pattern formation in nonlinear phenomena Thanks: AMS-2020, 35R03, 35R11
Francesca Corni, Fausto Ferrari

Abstract
. In this paper we construct the fractional powers of the sub-Laplacian in Carnot groups through an analytic continuation approach. In addition, we characterize the powers of the fractional sub-Laplacian in the Heisenberg group, and as a byproduct we compute the k k -th order momenta with respect to the heat kernel.



1.
Introduction

The fractional Laplacian may be considered as the simplest example of a non-local fractional integral operator. A great interest about this object arises, since it allows to model various phenomena related to different fields. It is well known that fractional operators are connected to potential theory, probability, harmonic and fractional analysis, pseudo-differential operators and partial differential equations, see for instance
[
13
]
,
[
35
]
,
[
10
]
and
[
19
]
.

On the other side, the setting of stratified groups represents a generalization of the Euclidean one, endowed with a rich structure, both from the algebraic and geometrical point of view.

Actually, the goal of generalizing fractional operators to stratified groups gives rise to appealing questions and open problems.
The aim of this paper is to investigate the connection between the fractional sub-Laplacian and suitable generalized Riesz potentials in the context of Carnot groups.

In particular, following an idea recalled in the book by Landkof,
[
27
]
, based on the work of Marcel Riesz,
[
33
]
,
[
34
]
, we propose a natural extension of the definition of the real powers of the sub-Laplacian on a stratified group based on a suitable analytic continuation approach. We would like to represent an alternative face to the problem already dealt with by Folland
[
14
]
.

Indeed, we think that this approach is very interesting for its simplicity and it is worth to be focused.

We start by considering a stratified group
\mathbb{G}
, i.e. a nilpotent simply connected Lie group whose Lie algebra admits a stratification. It is a standard fact that
\mathbb{G}
can be represented as a vector space endowed with both a structure of stratified Lie algebra, with grading

(1)

\mathbb{G}=V_{1}\oplus\ldots V_{\iota},

and Lie group.

We denote by
q
and
Q=\sum_{i=1}^{\iota}i\dim(V_{i})
the topological and the homogeneous dimension of
\mathbb{G}
, respectively, and, following
[
14
]
, we suppose that
Q\geq 3
. We assume that a homogeneous distance
d
on
\mathbb{G}
is fixed, i.e. a distance
d
such that
d(zx,zy)=d(x,y)
and
d(\delta_{t}(x),\delta_{t}(y))=td(x,y)
for every
x,y,z\in\mathbb{G}
and
t>0
, where
\delta_{t}
is a semigroup anisotropic dilation associated with the grading (
1
) of
\mathbb{G}
.

We denote by
m_{1}
the dimension of the first layer
V_{1}
of the grading. Moreover, we assume that a scalar product on
\mathbb{G}
is fixed and we consider an orthonormal basis
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{m_{1}})
of
V_{1}
. We set for every
i=1,\ldots,m_{1}
the vector field
X_{i}\in\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{G})
such that
X_{i}(0)=e_{i}
. The sub-Laplacian associated with
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{m_{1}})
is the operator

\mathcal{L}=-\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}X_{i}^{2}.

It is known that
\mathcal{L}
is hypoelliptic,
[
25
]
, left-invariant and
2
-homogeneous with respect to the anisotropic dilations
\{\delta_{t}\}_{t>0}
.

Fractional powers
\mathcal{L}^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
of
\mathcal{L}
, for
\alpha\in(-2,0)
, have been introduced through the theory of semigroups, involving the fundamental solution
h
of the heat operator
\mathcal{H}=\partial_{t}+\mathcal{L}
, that is the heat kernel on
\mathbb{G}
of
\mathcal{H}
(see Section
2.3
for definitions).

The operator
\mathcal{L}^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
,
\alpha\in(-2,0)
takes the name of fractional sub-Laplacian and it has been studied by many authors.

In
[
11
]
the authors established a pointwise integral representation of
\mathcal{L}^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}\phi(x)
through a family of suitable generalized Riesz
(\alpha-Q)
-homogeneous kernels
P_{\alpha}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}\setminus\{0\})
.
They showed that for every
\alpha\in(-2,0)
if
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
, then
\mathcal{L}^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{G})
and

(2)

\mathcal{L}^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}u(x)=P.V.\int_{\mathbb{G}}(u(y)-u(x))P_{\alpha}(x^{-1}y)dy.

We start by considering the operator

(3)

\phi\star P_{\alpha}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)P_{\alpha}(y)dy,

for
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
and
x\in\mathbb{G}
, where the symbol
\star
in (
4
) denotes the convolution in the group, and we notice that it is well defined for every
\alpha\in(0,Q)
.

The main result of our paper relies in the construction of an analytic continuation of the map

(4)

(0,Q)\ni\alpha\to\phi\star P_{\alpha}(x)=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)P_{\alpha}(y)dy,

to the interval
(-2,Q)
for any fixed
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
. This allows to extend in a natural way the definition of the power
\mathcal{L}^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
on the Schwartz class
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
, for
\alpha\in(-2,Q)
.

In our setting, we need to rely on various tools of geometric measure theory. Among these, in particular, we exploit the coarea formula proved in
[
29
,
30
]

and the Eikonal equation for the Carnot-Carathéodory distance proved in
[
31
]
.

On the other hand, we take into account various properties of the heat kernel
h
associated with
\mathcal{H}
, developed in
[
14
]
and
[
12
]
. Actually, the main step in our constuction is the estimate
[
3
, Theorem 20.3.3]
, involving homogeneous Taylor polynomials on a stratified group. In fact, while in the Euclidean setting the main point of the continuation is the Pizzetti formula, in an arbitrary stratified group the Pizzetti formula is not available yet. More precisely, even if some results are available in the literature
[
4
]
,
[
2
]
, they don’t seem directly exploitable in our approach.

In the second part of the paper, we restrict ourselves to the simplest non-trivial case, given by the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{n}
. We show by induction the construction of the analytic continuation of the map in (
4
) up to

(-\infty,Q)\ni\alpha\to\phi\star P_{\alpha}(x),

for any fixed
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
. In a natural way, this leads to an extension of the definition of
\mathcal{L}^{-\frac{\alpha}{2}}
on the Schwartz class
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{H}^{n})
, for
\alpha\in(-\infty,Q)
.

From our point of view, the Heisenberg group represents a privileged setting. In fact, in
\mathbb{H}^{n}
, both the heat kernel
h
, the kernels
P_{\alpha}

and the profile of the Carnot-Carathéodory ball
\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)

preserve various symmetries related to the structure of the group.
In an arbitrary stratified group it is not clear how to proceed.

These invariances allow to rely on the use of Taylor polynomials to extend the continuation of the map in (
4
) to every strip
(-2m-2,-2m]
, with
m\in\mathbb{N}
, through an inductive procedure.

Eventually, we provide simplified representations of the extended map
\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)
on the intervals
(-4,Q)
,
(-6,Q)
and
(-8,Q)
. By combining these representations with
[
12
, Proposition 1.5]
, we obtain several geometric consequences. In particular, we compute the value of the integrals

(5)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}x_{i}^{2}h(1,x)dx&\quad\int_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}x_{i}^{4}h(1,x)dx\quad\int_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}x_{2n+1}^{2}h(1,x)dx\\
\int_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}x_{i}^{6}h(1,x)&dx\quad\int_{\mathbb{H}^{n}}x_{i}^{2}x_{2n+1}^{2}h(1,x)dx,\end{split}

for
i\in\{1,\ldots,2n\}
, that of course have an important probabilistic meaning.

Now, we briefly describe the content of the various sections. In Section
2
we introduce stratified groups and we collect many related definitions and results available in literature. Moreover, we deal with the sub-Laplacian
\mathcal{L}
and fractional sub-Laplacian
\mathcal{L}^{s}
, with
s\in(0,1)
and we introduce the family of the generalized Riesz potentials
P_{\alpha}
.

In Section
3
we study the map

(0,Q)\ni\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)=\phi\star P_{\alpha}(x),

and we build up its analytic continuation to the strip
(-2,0]
. Hence, we are in position to extend the definition of the fractional sub-Laplacian
\mathcal{L}^{s}
to
s\in(-\frac{Q}{2},1)
.

In Section
4
we sketch the setting of the Heisenberg group
\mathbb{H}^{n}
for fixing notation and we show some technical auxiliary results.

In Section
5
we build up the analytic continuation of the function
\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)
on the interval
(-\infty,Q)
. Then, we exploit the uniqueness of the analytic continuation to extend the notion of fractional sub-Laplacian
\mathcal{L}^{s}
to every
s\in(-\frac{Q}{2},\infty)
.

Finally, in Sections
6
,
7
and
8
we obtain simplified representations of
\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)
on the intervals
(-4,Q)
,
(-6,Q)
and
(-8,Q)
, respectively. We apply these representations to establish the value of the integrals in (
5
).

2.
Preliminaries

2.1.
Stratified groups, Pansu differentiabily, coarea formula

In this section we collect some definitions and more or less known results about stratified groups, that we state for fixing the notation.

Definition 2.1
.

A
stratified group
or
Carnot group

\mathbb{G}
of step
\iota
is a connected, simply connected and nilpotent Lie group whose Lie algebra is stratified of step
\iota
, i.e. there exists a sequence of subspaces
\mathcal{V}_{j}
with
j\in\mathbb{N}
, such that
\mathcal{V}_{j}=\{0\}
if
j>\iota
,
[\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{i}]=\mathcal{V}_{i+1}
for every
i,j\geq 1
,
\mathcal{V}_{\iota}\neq\{0\}
and

\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{G})=\mathcal{V}_{1}\oplus\dots\oplus\mathcal{V}_{\iota}
,
where for
i,j\geq 1
we have set

[\mathcal{V}_{i},\mathcal{V}_{j}]=\text{span}\{[X,Y]:X\in\mathcal{V}_{i},\ Y\in\mathcal{V}_{j}\}.

The exponential map
\mathrm{exp}:\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{G})\to\mathbb{G}
is a global diffeomorphism which allows to identify in a standard way
\mathbb{G}
with
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{G})
(see for instance
[
3
, Section 2.2]
).
As a consequence, we can model a stratified group as a vector space
\mathbb{G}
endowed with both a Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
with respect to which
\mathbb{G}
is a stratified Lie algebra

(6)

\mathbb{G}=V_{1}\oplus V_{2}\ldots\oplus V_{\iota},

and a Lie group structure, whose product is given by the Baker-Campbell-Hausdorff operation associated with
[\cdot,\cdot]
(see
[
3
, Theorem 2.2.1, Theorem 2.2.13, Corollary 2.2.15, Theorem 2.2.24]
).

For every
x\in\mathbb{G}
we denote the corresponding left translation by

l_{x}:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{G},\ l_{x}(y)=xy,

for every
y\in\mathbb{G}
. We denote by
H\mathbb{G}
the distribution generated by
V_{1}
, it is called the
horizontal distribution
.
A basis
(e_{1},\ldots,e_{q})
of
\mathbb{G}
is called
graded
if
the ordered family
(e_{h_{i-1}+1},\ldots,e_{h_{i}})
is a basis of
V_{i}
for every
i=1,\ldots,\iota
.
We call
(Z_{1},\cdots Z_{q})
a
Jacobian basis
of
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{G})
if
(Z_{1}(0),\ldots Z_{q}(0))
is a graded basis of
\mathbb{G}
.
A family of dilations is associated with the stratification of
\mathbb{G}
in a natural way: for every
t>0
we set

\delta_{t}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\iota}v_{i}\right)=\sum_{i=1}^{\iota}t^{i}v_{i}\qquad\mathrm{with}\ v_{i}\in V_{i}.

Definition 2.2
.

(Degree)
Let
v\in\mathbb{G}
, we call
n\in\mathbb{N}
the
degree
of
v
, and we write
\mathrm{deg}(v)=n
, if

\delta_{t}(v)=t^{n}v,

hence if
v\in V_{n}
. Analogously, for a vector field
X\in\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{G})
, we say that
\mathrm{deg}(X)=n
if
X\in\mathrm{Lie}(V_{n})
.

We assume that a scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is fixed on
\mathbb{G}

and we denote by
|\cdot|
its associated norm. Moreover, we assume that
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
makes the
V_{i}
’s orthogonal. Taking into account the structure of vector space of
\mathbb{G}
, we may identify it with
T_{0}\mathbb{G}
. Thus, the scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
extends through the left translations to a left invariant Riemannian metric that we denote by
g
.

A distance
d
on
\mathbb{G}
is called
homogeneous
if
d(zx,zy)=d(x,y)
and
d(\delta_{t}(x),\delta_{t}(y))=td(x,y)
for every
x,y,z\in\mathbb{G}
and
t>0
.
Let us recall two concrete examples of homogeneous distances (see
[
3
, Definition 5.2.2]
and
[
17
, Theorem 5.1]
).
First, we consider
x,y\in\mathbb{G}
and we set
\Gamma_{x,y}
the set of
horizontal curves
, i.e. absolutely continuous curves
\gamma:[0,T]\to\mathbb{G}
with
\dot{\gamma(t)}\in H\mathbb{G}_{\gamma(t)}
for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
for some
T>0
, such that
\gamma(0)=x,\ \gamma(T)=y
.
The
Carnot-Carathéodory distance
or CC-distance between
x
and
y
is

d_{c}(x,y)=\inf\left\{\int^{T}_{0}\sqrt{g(\gamma(t))(\dot{\gamma}(t),\dot{\gamma}(t))}dt:\gamma\in\Gamma_{x,y}\right\}.

For every
x,y\in\mathbb{G}
, the Chow’s theorem ensures that the set
\Gamma_{x,y}
is non-empty hence
d_{c}
is well defined. We denote by
\|x\|_{c}=d(x,0)
, for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
, the homogeneous norm associated with
d_{c}
.
The second homogeneous distance we introduce is the distance
d_{\infty}
, defined for all
x,y\in\mathbb{G}
as

d_{\infty}(x,y)=\|y^{-1}x\|_{\infty},

where the homogeneous norm
\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
is

\|x\|_{\infty}:=\max\{\varepsilon_{j}|x^{j}|^{1/j},\ j=1,\dots,\kappa\},

for
x=\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}x^{j}\in\mathbb{G}
, with
x^{j}\in V_{j}
, with
\varepsilon_{1}=1
,
\varepsilon_{j}\in(0,1]
suitable positive constants depending on the group structure .
In this paper, we assume that a homogeneous distance
d
is fixed on
\mathbb{G}
and we denote by
\|x\|=d(x,0)
, for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
, the corresponding homogeneous norm. Moreover, we introduce the following closed balls

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}(x,r)

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathbb{G}:\|x\|\leq r\},

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{E}(x,r)

\displaystyle=\{x\in\mathbb{G}:|x|\leq r\},

for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
r>0
.
The closed ball centered at
x
of radius
r
corresponding to
d_{c}
will be denoted as
\mathbb{B}_{c}(x,r)
.
We recall that all homogeneous norms on
\mathbb{G}
are equivalent (see for instance
[
3
, Theorem 5.1.4]
), hence if
d_{1}
,
d_{2}
are two homogeneous distances on
\mathbb{G}
, then there exist a positive constant
C>0
such that

\frac{1}{C}d_{1}(x,y)\leq d_{2}(x,y)\leq Cd_{1}(x,y),

for every
x,y\in\mathbb{G}
.
We denote by
q
the topological (or linear) dimension of
\mathbb{G}
and we call
homogeneous dimension
of
\mathbb{G}
the integer

Q=\sum_{i=1}^{\iota}i\dim(V_{i}),

which is also the Hausdorff dimension of
\mathbb{G}
with respect to a homogeneous distance
d
. We assume that
Q\geq 3
.
Let us recall also the notion of Hausdorff measure in this context. Let
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{G})
be a non-empty family of closed subsets of a Carnot group
\mathbb{G}
, endowed with a homogeneous distance
d
. Let
\zeta:\mathcal{F}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
be a function such that
0\leq\zeta(S)<\infty
for any
S\in\mathcal{F}
. If
\delta>0
, and
A\subset\mathbb{G}
, we define

(7)

\phi_{\delta,\zeta}(A)=\inf\left\{\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\zeta(B_{j})\ :\ A\subset\bigcup_{j=0}^{\infty}B_{j},\ \mathrm{diam}(B_{j})\leq\delta,\ B_{j}\in\mathcal{F}\right\}.

If
\mathcal{F}
coincides with the family of closed balls with respect to the distance
d
and
\zeta(\mathbb{B}(x,r))=r^{\alpha}
we call

\mathcal{S}^{\alpha}(A):=\sup_{\delta>0}\phi_{\delta,\zeta}(A)

the
\alpha
-spherical Hausdorff measure
of
A
.
If
\mathcal{F}
coincides with the family of all
closed sets, and for
k\in\{1,\dots,q\}
we set

c_{k}=\omega_{k}
, the
k
-dimensional Euclidean measure of the unitary Euclidean ball in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, and
\zeta(B)=c_{k}\left(\frac{\mathrm{diam}(B)}{2}\right)^{k},
we call

\mathcal{H}^{k}_{E}(A):=\sup_{\delta>0}\phi_{\delta,\zeta}(A)

the
k
-Euclidean Hausdorff measure
.
In particular, we stress that
\mathcal{H}_{E}^{k}
is the Hausdorff measure with respect to the distance generated by the Riemannian metric
g
fixed on
\mathbb{G}
. Nevertheless, if we consider
\mathbb{G}
in coordinates with respect to an orthonormal basis, then
\mathcal{H}_{E}^{k}
coincides with the Euclidean Hausdorff measure and this justify our notation.
The
q
-dimensional Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{G}
is the Haar measure of
\mathbb{G}
. It is left invariant and
Q
-homogeneous.
On the other hand, for every
m>0
, for every measurable set
A\subset\mathbb{G}
and for all
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
t>0
we have

\mathcal{S}^{m}(l_{x}(A))=\mathcal{S}^{m}(A)\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\mathcal{S}^{m}(\delta_{t}(A))=t^{m}\mathcal{S}^{m}(A).

We refer the reader for example to
[
36
]
for more details about an introduction to stratified groups.

2.1.1.
Pansu differentiability

A map
L:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
h-homomorphism
if it is a group homomorphism such that
L(\delta_{t}(x))=t(L(x))
, for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
t>0
. It is not difficult to verify that
L
is linear and
L|_{V_{j}}\equiv 0
for every
j=2,\ldots,\iota
. We denote by
\mathcal{L}_{h}(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{R})
the family of h-homomorphisms between
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{R}
.
Given
L,T\in\mathcal{L}_{h}(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{R})
, we define the distance

d_{\mathcal{L}_{h}(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{R})}(L,T):=\sup_{\|x\|\leq 1}|T(x)^{-1}L(x)|.

This class of maps is the starting point to state a notion of differentiability fitting the setting of stratified groups.
Let
\Omega
be an open set in
\mathbb{G}
, let
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be a function and let
x\in\Omega
.
The map
f
is said
Pansu differentiable
at
x
if there exists a h-homomorphism
L:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}
that satisfies

(8)

|f(y)-f(x)-L(x^{-1}y)|=o(\|x^{-1}y\|)\quad\text{as}\quad\|x^{-1}y\|\to 0.

When condition (
8
) is verified, we call
L
the
Pansu differential
of
f
at
x
and we denote it by
D_{h}f(x)
.
The unique vector
\nabla_{H}f(x)\in\mathbb{G}
such that

(9)

D_{h}f(x)(z)=\left\langle\nabla_{H}f(x),z\right\rangle

for every
z\in\mathbb{G}
is called the
horizontal gradient
of
f
at
x
.
Assuming that
(v_{1},\dots,v_{q})
is a graded orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{G}
and setting
X_{j}\in\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{G})
the left invariant vector field such that
X_{j}(0)=v_{j}
for every
j=1,\dots,q

(10)

\nabla_{H}f(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}X_{i}f(x)v_{i},

where
m_{1}=\dim(V_{1})
(see
[
36
, Proposition 3.10]
and the references therein).
We say that
f:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}

is
continuously Pansu differentiable
on
\Omega
if
f
is Pansu differentiable at every point of
\Omega
and the function

D_{h}f:\Omega\to\mathcal{L}_{h}(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{R})
is continuous with respect to the topology induced by
d_{\mathcal{L}_{h}(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{R})}
.
We denote by
C^{1}_{h}(\Omega)
the family of continuously Pansu differentiable mappings from
\Omega
to
\mathbb{R}
and we observe that
C^{1}(\Omega)\subsetneq C^{1}_{h}(\Omega)
.

2.1.2.
Coarea formula in stratified groups

In this subsection we recall a suitable coarea formula in Carnot groups. It is interesting to notice that the starting point of the proof of Theorem
2.4
is a previous coarea formula for bounded variation maps (
[
16
,
20
,
31
]
).

Definition 2.3
.

Let
v\in V_{1}\setminus\{0\}
be a vector and consider its orthogonal hyperplane
\mathcal{L}(v)\subset\mathbb{G}
. We define

\theta^{g}_{Q-1}(v):=\max_{z\in\mathbb{B}(0,1)}\mathcal{H}_{E}^{q-1}(\mathcal{L}(v)\cap\mathbb{B}(z,1))

the
metric factor
with respect to the direction
v
.

We explicitly remark that
\theta^{g}_{Q-1}(v)
depends both on the homogeneous distance
d
and on the Riemannian metric
g
.

The following theorem is proved in
[
29
, Theorem 3.5]
(see also
[
30
]
).

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
A\subset\mathbb{G}
be a measurable set and consider a Lipschitz function
u:A\to\mathbb{R}
. For any measurable function
h:A\to[0,\infty)
the equality

\int_{A}h(x)|\nabla_{H}u(x)|dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{u^{-1}(r)\cap A}h(x)\theta^{g}_{Q-1}(\nabla_{H}u(x))d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}(x)dr

holds. Notice that
\theta^{g}_{Q-1}(\nabla_{H}u(x))
and
\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}

are both considered with respect to
d
.

On the other hand, by
[
29
, Proposition 1.9]
and
[
30
, Theorem 5.2]
we know that the distance
d_{\infty}
preserves enough symmetries to guarantee the constancy of the metric factor. This is the content of the following proposition.

Proposition 2.5
.

If
d=d_{\infty}
, then there is a constant
\alpha_{Q-1}>0
such that

\theta^{g}_{Q-1}(v)=\alpha_{Q-1},\qquad\mathrm{for\ every}\ v\in V_{1}\setminus\{0\}.

Thus, in the setting of Proposition
2.5
, we may set

\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}:=\alpha_{Q-1}\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}

to yield a simplified coarea formula, which follows from the combination of
[
29
, Corollary 3.6]
and Proposition
2.5
.

Theorem 2.6
.

Let
d=d_{\infty}
, let
A\subset\mathbb{G}
be a measurable set and let
u:A\to\mathbb{R}

be a Lipschitz function. For any measurable function
h:A\to[0,\infty)
, the following equality holds

\int_{A}h(x)|\nabla_{H}u(x)|dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{u^{-1}(r)\cap A}h(x)d\mathcal{S}_{\infty}^{Q-1}dr.

Now, in order to obtain a further simplified expression of the coarea formula, we recall the Eikonal equation associates with the CC-distance
d_{c}
, which is a consequence of
[
31
, Theorem 3.1]
.

Theorem 2.7
.

For a.e.
x\in\mathbb{G}
the equality

|\nabla_{H}d_{c}(x,0)|=1
holds.

By combining Theorems
2.6
and Theorem
2.7
, setting
u(x):\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R},\ u(x)=d_{c}(x,0)
as the Lipschitz function in Theorem
2.6
, we get the following formula, which retraces the Euclidean coarea formula.

Corollary 2.8
.

(Coarea formula)

If
d=d_{\infty}
and
A\subset\mathbb{G}
is a measurable set,
then for any measurable function
h:A\to[0,\infty)
we have

\int_{A}h(x)dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,r)\cap A}h(x)d\mathcal{S}_{\infty}^{Q-1}dr.

2.2.
Taylor polynomial on stratified groups

In this subsection we introduce homogeneous Taylor polinomials on
\mathbb{G}
. We mainly follow
[
3
, Chapter 20]
.
We choose
m\in\mathbb{N}
and for every
m
-multi-index
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m})\in(\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})^{m}
we set

|\gamma|=\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}+\dots\gamma_{m}\qquad\gamma!=\gamma_{1}!\gamma_{2}!\dots\gamma_{m}!\qquad\mathrm{and}\qquad x^{\gamma}=x_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}x_{2}^{\gamma_{2}}\dots x_{m}^{\gamma_{m}}\quad\mathrm{for\ every\ }x\in\mathbb{R}^{m}.

Moreover, we introduce

(Z_{1},\cdots,Z_{m})^{\gamma}=Z_{1}^{\gamma_{1}}\cdots Z_{m}^{\gamma_{m}},\quad\mathrm{for\ every\ }Z_{1},\ldots Z_{m}\in\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{G}).

We say that an
m
-multi-index
\gamma=(\gamma_{1},\ldots,\gamma_{m})\in(\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})^{m}
is
even
if
\gamma_{j}
is even (0 being even) for every
j\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
.

If
f\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{R})
and
(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{q})
is a Jacobian basis of
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{G})
, then for every
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
there exists one and only one polynomial
p
homogeneous of degree at most
n
such that

(11)

(Z_{1}\cdots Z_{q})^{\beta}(p)(0)=(Z_{1}\cdots Z_{q})^{\beta}(f)(0),

for every multi-index
\beta\in(\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\})^{q}
such that
|\beta|\leq n
.
The unique polynomial satisfying (
11
) is denoted by
\mathcal{T}_{n}(f,0)(x)
and it is called the
Z
-Mac Laurin polynomial homogeneous of degree
n
related to
f
.
Then, for every
x,x_{0}\in\mathbb{G}
we introduce the so-called
Z
-Taylor polynomial of
\mathbb{G}
-degree
n
related to
f
centered at
x_{0}
as

(12)

\mathcal{T}_{n}(f,x_{0})(x):=\mathcal{T}_{n}(f\circ l_{x_{0}},0)(x_{0}^{-1}x).

Let us recall an important estimate involving
Z
-Taylor polynomials, obtained by
[
3
, Theorem 20.3.3]
, setting
d=d_{c}
(see also
[
15
, Theorem 1.37]
).

Theorem 2.9
.

(Stratified Taylor formula)

Let
\mathbb{G}
be a stratified group. There is a constant
b=b(\mathbb{G})
such that for every
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, there exists a constant
c=c(\mathbb{G},n)>0
such that

(13)

|f(xy)-\mathcal{T}_{n}(f,x)(xy)|\leq c\|y\|_{c}^{n+1}\sup_{\{\|z\|_{c}\leq b^{n+1}\|y\|_{c}\}}\sup_{\{|\beta|=n+1\}}|(Z_{1}\cdots Z_{m_{1}})^{\beta}f(xz)|,

for all
f\in C^{n+1}(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{R})
, for every
x,y\in\mathbb{G}
. Here
\beta
is a
m_{1}
-multi-index and
m_{1}=\dim(V_{1})
.

An explicit form of the
Z
-Taylor polynomial homogeneous of
\mathbb{G}
-degree
n
is known. We refer the reader to
[
3
, Corollary 20.3.15]
(see also
[
5
, Theorem 2]
formula (43)).

Theorem 2.10
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
, let
f\in C^{n+1}(\mathbb{G},\mathbb{R})
and choose
x_{0}\in\mathbb{G}
. Let
(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{q})
be a Jacobian basis of
\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{G})
, then

\mathcal{T}_{n}(f,x_{0})(x)=f(x_{0})+\sum_{h=1}^{n}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=1,\ldots,n\\
i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\leq q,\\
\mathrm{deg}(Z_{i_{1}})+\ldots\mathrm{deg}(Z_{i_{k}})=h\end{subarray}}\frac{(x_{0}^{-1}x)_{i_{1}}\ldots(x_{0}^{-1}x)_{i_{k}}}{k!}Z_{i_{1}}\ldots Z_{i_{k}}f(x_{0}),

for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
.

2.3.
Fractional sub-Laplacian on stratified groups

In this subsection, we introduce the sub-Laplacian on
\mathbb{G}
and its powers. We choose a graded orthonormal basis
(v_{1},\dots,v_{m_{1}})
of
V_{1}
and we let
X_{i}\in\mathrm{Lie}(\mathbb{G})
be the left invariant vector field such that
X_{i}(0)=v_{i}
for every
i=1,\dots,m_{1}
.
We call
sub-Laplacian
of
\mathbb{G}
the second-order differential operator

(14)

\mathcal{L}:=-\sum_{i=1}^{m_{1}}X_{i}^{2}.

It is a positive operator associated with the choice of the basis
(v_{1},\ldots,v_{m_{1}})
.

Remark 2.11
.

Since
\mathbb{G}
is a stratified group, by Hormander’s theorem the operator
\mathcal{L}
is hypoelliptic. By
[
14
, Theorem 2.1]
there exists a unique fundamental solution for
\mathcal{L}
.

Let
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
be the homogeneous group

\widehat{\mathbb{G}}=\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{G},

endowed with the product

(t,x)\cdot(t^{\prime}.y)=(t+t^{\prime},xy)

and the following stratification

\widehat{\mathbb{G}}=V_{1}\oplus\widehat{V_{2}}\oplus\dots\oplus V_{\iota},\quad\mathrm{with}\quad\mathrm{Lie}(\widehat{V_{2}})=\mathrm{span}\left\{\frac{\partial}{\partial t},\mathrm{Lie}(V_{2})\right\}.

The group
\widehat{G}
may be equipped with the dilations

(15)

\widehat{\delta}_{s}(t,x)=(s^{2}t,\delta_{s}(x)),

for every
s>0
. We consider on
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
the heat operator

\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{L}+\frac{\partial}{\partial t},

where

\mathcal{L}
is the sub-Laplacian in (
14
).
The operator
\mathcal{H}
is invariant with respect to the left translations of
\widehat{\mathbb{G}}
and homogeneous of degree 2 with respect to the dilations in (
15
), i.e.
\mathcal{H}(u\circ\widehat{\delta}_{s})=s^{2}\mathcal{H}u
for
s>0
. Moreover,
\mathcal{H}
is hypoelliptic and it has a unique fundamental solution
h
called the
heat kernel
on
\mathbb{G}
(see
[
14
]
). We collect some properties of
h
addressing the reader to
[
15
, Proposition 1.68]
,
[
12
, Theorem 1.1]
and
[
15
, Proposition 1.74]
and to the references therein.

Theorem 2.12
.

Let
h:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}
be the heat kernel associated with the sub-Laplacian
\mathcal{L}
on
\mathbb{G}
, then the following conditions hold

(i)

h\in C^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}\setminus\{(0,0)\})
.

(ii)

\mathcal{H}h=0
on
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{G}
.

(iii)

\int_{\mathbb{G}}h(t,x)dx=1
, for every
t>0
;
h(t,x)=h(t,x^{-1})
,
h(t,x)\geq 0
, for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
h(t,x)=0
, when
t\leq 0
.

(iv)

h(t,\cdot)\star h(\tau,\cdot)=h(t+\tau,\cdot)
, for every
t,\tau>0
.

(v)

h(\widehat{\delta}_{s}(t,x))=s^{-Q}h(t,x)
, for every
s,t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{G}
.

(vi)

There exists a constant
c>0
such that

c^{-1}t^{-Q/2}\mathrm{exp}\Big(-\frac{\|x\|^{2}}{c^{-1}t}\Big)\leq h(t,x)\leq ct^{-Q/2}\mathrm{exp}\Big(-\frac{\|x\|^{2}}{ct}\Big),

for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
t>0
.

(vii)

h(t,\cdot)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
, for every
t>0
.

Now, we introduce the
heat semigroup
:

(16)

e^{-t\mathcal{L}}f(x):=(f\star h(t,\cdot))(x)=\int_{\mathbb{G}}h(t,y^{-1}x)f(y)dy,

for every
f\in L^{1}(\mathbb{G})
, where
\star
denotes the group convolution.
We define the fractional sub-Laplacian
\mathcal{L}^{s}
,
0<s<1
as follows (see
[
37
]
). For every map
u\in W^{2s,2}(\mathbb{G})
and for every
x\in\mathbb{G}

(17)

\mathcal{L}^{s}u(x):=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-s-1}(e^{-t\mathcal{L}}u(x)-u(x))dt.

See
[
12
, Section 1.2]
and the references therein for the definition and an introduction to fractional Sobolev space on a stratified group. The operator
\mathcal{L}^{s}
is non-local.

We recall the following result about the behaviour of
\mathcal{L}^{s}
, when
s
is close to one, see also
[
12
, Proposition 1.5]
. We remind the proof of the theorem to point out some technical aspects of the result. By
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
we denote the Schwartz class on
\mathbb{G}
(see
[
15
, Section 1.D]
).

Proposition 2.13
.

For every
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
and for every
x\in\mathbb{G}

\lim_{s\to 1^{-}}\mathcal{L}^{s}\phi(x)=\mathcal{L}\phi(x).

Proof.

Using the change of variables
\xi=t^{1-s}
on (
17
), we get

(18)

\mathcal{L}^{s}\phi(x)=\frac{1}{(1-s)\Gamma(-s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}(e^{-\xi^{\frac{1}{1-s}\mathcal{L}}}\phi(x)-\phi(x))\xi^{-\frac{1}{1-s}}d\xi.

Clearly, if
0\leq\xi<1
then
\lim_{s\to 1^{-}}\xi^{\frac{1}{1-s}}=0
. Since
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
, by
[
14
, Theorem 3.1(ii)]
(notice that
\mathcal{D(\mathbb{G})}
is
L^{\infty}
-dense in
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
) we have

(19)

\left\|\frac{e^{-h\mathcal{L}}\phi-\phi}{h}+\mathcal{L}\phi\right\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\to 0\ \mathrm{as}\ h\to 0.

Set
c(s)=\frac{1}{(1-s)\Gamma(-s)}=\frac{-s}{(1-s)\Gamma(1-s)}
. We claim that for every
x\in\mathbb{G}

(20)

\lim_{s\to 1^{-}}c(s)\int_{0}^{1}(e^{-\xi^{\frac{1}{1-s}\mathcal{L}}}\phi(x)-\phi(x))\xi^{-\frac{1}{1-s}}d\xi=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{L}(x).

Indeed, denoting by
f_{s,x}(\xi):=((e^{-\xi^{\frac{1}{1-s}\mathcal{L}}}\phi(x)-\phi(x))\xi^{-\frac{1}{1-s}}
and using (
19
) we get

(21)

f_{s,x}(\xi)\to-\mathcal{L}(x)

for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
. Moreover,

(22)

\begin{split}|f_{s,x}(\xi)|&=\left|((e^{-\xi^{\frac{1}{1-s}\mathcal{L}}}\phi(x)-\phi(x))\xi^{-\frac{1}{1-s}}\right|=\left|\frac{1}{\xi^{\frac{1}{1-s}}}\int_{0}^{\xi^{\frac{1}{1-s}}}\frac{d}{d\tau}e^{-\tau\mathcal{L}}\phi(x)d\tau\right|\\
&=\left|\frac{1}{\xi^{\frac{1}{1-s}}}\int_{0}^{\xi^{\frac{1}{1-s}}}e^{-\tau\mathcal{L}}\mathcal{L}\phi(x)d\tau\right|\leq\|\mathcal{L}\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})},\end{split}

where in the last inequality we used that
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
and the fact, proved in
[
6
, Proposition 3.2.1]
, that the heat semigroup preserves one-sided bounds, namely

(23)

f\leq C\mathrm{\ a.e.\ in\ }\mathbb{G}\Rightarrow e^{-t\mathcal{L}}f\leq C\mathrm{\ a.e.\ in\ }\mathbb{G}\qquad\forall t\geq 0.

Thus, our claim (
20
) follows using the definition of
c(s)
, hence from the fact that
\lim_{s\to 1^{-}}c(s)=-1
, and from (
21
) and (
22
). Furthermore, using (
23
), we obtain

\displaystyle c(s)\int_{1}^{\infty}|e^{-\xi^{\frac{1}{1-s}}\mathcal{L}}\phi(x)-\phi(x)|\xi^{-\frac{1}{1-s}}d\xi

\displaystyle\leq 2c(s)\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\int_{1}^{\infty}\xi^{-\frac{1}{1-s}}d\xi

\displaystyle=-2\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\frac{-s}{(1-s)\Gamma(1-s)}\frac{1-s}{-s}

\displaystyle=-2\frac{1}{\Gamma(1-s)}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\to 0

as
s\to 1^{-}
. Thus, letting
s\to 1^{-}
we get the thesis.
∎

2.4.
Generalized Riesz potentials

In this section we introduce a family of generalized Riesz potentials.
For every
\alpha<Q
,
\alpha\neq 0,-2,-4,\cdots
we set
P_{\alpha}:\mathbb{G}\to\mathbb{R}
as

P_{\alpha}(x)=\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)dt.

We observe that
P_{\alpha}
coincides with the map
R_{\alpha}
defined in
[
14
,
11
,
12
]
for
0<\alpha<Q
.
We collect below some properties of the
P_{\alpha}
(see
[
14
, Proposition 3.17]
).

(1)

By Theorem
2.12
(vi), for every
x\neq 0

h(t,x)=o(t^{N})\ \mathrm{as}\ t\to 0^{+}

for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
. For every
x\in\mathbb{G}
,

h(t,x)\leq ct^{-\frac{Q}{2}}

for some constant
c\geq 0
and for every
t>0
.

Thus,
P_{\alpha}(x)
converges absolutely if
\alpha<Q
for every
x\neq 0
.

(2)

For every
\alpha<Q
,
P_{\alpha}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G}\setminus\{0\})
, since
h(t,x)\in C^{\infty}(\widehat{\mathbb{G}}\setminus\{(0,0)\})
.

(3)

For every
0<\alpha<Q
,
P_{\alpha}
is non-negative. Moreover, for every
\alpha<Q
it is homogeneous of degree
\alpha-Q
with respect to the intrinsic dilations
\delta_{s}
,
s>0
. Let us show the homogeneity, for
s>0
we have

\displaystyle P_{\alpha}(\delta_{s}(x))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,\delta_{s}(x))dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h\left(\frac{1}{s^{2}}s^{2}t,\delta_{s}(x)\right)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}s^{-Q}h\left(\frac{1}{s^{2}}t,x\right)dt,

where we exploited Theorem
2.12
(v). Let us now perform the change of variables
t=s^{2}\tau
so that we get

\displaystyle P_{\alpha}(\delta_{s}(x))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}(s^{2}\tau)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}s^{-Q}h(\tau,x)s^{2}d\tau

\displaystyle=s^{\alpha-2-Q+2}\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\tau^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(\tau,x)d\tau

\displaystyle=s^{\alpha-Q}P_{\alpha}(x).

We introduce for every
\alpha<Q
and for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
,
x\neq 0
, the function

(24)

\|x\|_{\alpha}:=\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)dt\right)^{\frac{1}{\alpha-Q}}.

Notice that for every
\alpha<Q
,
\alpha\neq 0,-2,-4,\ldots
and
x\in\mathbb{G}
,
x\neq 0
, the equality

P_{\alpha}(x)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\|x\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}

holds.
The map
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}
is a homogeneous norm on
\mathbb{G}
smooth outside the identity element (see
[
12
, Section 1.1]
and Item
3
in Section
2.4
). Since on a Carnot group every homogeneous norms are equivalent, for every
\alpha<Q
there exists a constant
c_{\alpha}>0
such that

(25)

\frac{1}{c_{\alpha}}\|x\|_{c}\leq\|x\|_{\alpha}\leq c_{\alpha}\|x\|_{c},

for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
.

Remark 2.14
.

Let us verify that if
0<\alpha<Q
, then
P_{\alpha}\in L^{1}_{loc}(\mathbb{G})
. In fact, by combining the coarea formula of Corollary
2.8
, the homogeneity of the measure
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}^{Q-1}
and the homogeneity of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}
, for every
R>0
we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R)}\|x\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{0}^{R}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,r)}\|x\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(x)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{0}^{R}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}r^{\alpha-Q}\|z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}r^{Q-1}d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(z)dt

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{0}^{R}r^{\alpha-1}dr\ \mathcal{S}_{\infty}^{Q-1}(\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1))\max_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\|z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q},

which is finite since
\alpha>0
.

Taking into account the previous Items
2
,
3
and Remark
2.14
, it holds that if
0<\alpha<Q
, then
P_{\alpha}
is a kernel of type
\alpha
, according to the definition in
[
14
]
. We list below various results known in the literature that relate the maps
P_{\alpha}
with
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{L}^{s}
, for
0<s<1
. We give the details of their own proofs only in those cases that are useful for the sequel of the paper.

(i)

The map
P_{2}
is the fundamental solution of
\mathcal{L}
. Indeed for
x\in\mathbb{G}

\mathcal{L}P_{2}(x)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{L}h(t,x)dt=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(t,x)dt=-\lim_{t\to\infty}h(t,x)+h(0,x)=-\lim_{t\to\infty}h(t,x)=0,

where we exploited Theorem
2.12
(iii),(vi) and the known estimates about the derivatives of the heat kernel (see for example the proof of
[
15
, Proposition 1.75]
)

(ii)

By
[
21
, Proposition 4.1]
, for every
s\in(0,1)
the kernel
P_{2s}
is the fundamental solution of
\mathcal{L}^{s}
.

(iii)

By
[
14
, Theorem 3.15(iii), Proposition 3.18]
and
[
18
, Lemma 8.5]
we can deduce that for every
s\in(0,1)
and
u\in C^{\infty}_{0}(\mathbb{G})
the equality

\mathcal{L}^{s}u=\mathcal{L}u\star P_{2-2s}

holds.

(iv)

For every
s\in(0,1)
the following relation holds outside the origin

\mathcal{L}P_{2-2s}=P_{-2s}.

In fact, let
x\in\mathbb{G}
,
x\neq 0

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}P_{2-2s}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}\left(\frac{1}{\Gamma\left(1-s\right)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{2-2s}{2}-1}h(t,x)dt\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{-s\Gamma(-s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-s}\mathcal{L}h(t,x)dt

\displaystyle=-\left(\frac{1}{-s\Gamma(-s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-s}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}h(t,x)dt\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{-s\Gamma(-s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}(-s)t^{-s-1}h(t,x)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(-s)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{-s-1}h(t,x)dt=P_{-2s}(x),

where we have considered as well the known estimates about the derivatives of the heat kernel (see
[
15
, Proposition 1.75]
) and the integration by parts.

(v)

Reading
[
11
, Theorem 3.11]
in light of item (iv), for every
s\in(0,1)
if
u\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
, then
\mathcal{L}^{s}u\in L^{2}(\mathbb{G})
and

(26)

\mathcal{L}^{s}u(x)=P.V.\int_{\mathbb{G}}(u(y)-u(x))P_{-2s}(x^{-1}y)dy.

We have taken into account the equivalence proved in
[
18
, Lemma 8.5]
.

(vi)

For every
0<\alpha,\beta<Q
such that
\alpha+\beta<Q
and for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
,
x\neq 0
the following relation holds

(27)

P_{\alpha+\beta}(x)=P_{\alpha}\star P_{\beta}(x).

Let us show (
27
):

\displaystyle P_{\alpha}\star P_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,y)dt\int_{0}^{\infty}\tau^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}h(\tau,y^{-1}x)d\tau dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\int_{(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\tau^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}h(t,y)h(\tau,y^{-1}x)dtd\tau dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2})}\int_{(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\tau^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}\int_{\mathbb{G}}h(t,y)h(\tau,y^{-1}x)dydtd\tau

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2})}\int_{(0,\infty)\times(0,\infty)}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\tau^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}h(t+\tau,x)dtd\tau,

where we used Fubini’s theorem and Theorem
2.12
(iv). Then, we consider the change of variables

\begin{cases}t=s(1-u)\\
\tau=us,\end{cases}

getting

\displaystyle P_{\alpha}\star P_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2})}\int_{(0,\infty)\times(0,1)}(s(1-u))^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}(us)^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}h(s,x)s\ dsdu

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}s^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}sh(s,x)ds\int_{0}^{1}(1-u)^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}u^{\frac{\beta}{2}-1}du

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}-1}h(s,x)ds\ B\left(\frac{\alpha}{2},\frac{\beta}{2}\right),

where
B(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the Beta Euler function. Exploiting the relation between the Beta and the Gamma Euler functions, we have

\displaystyle P_{\alpha}\star P_{\beta}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}-1}h(s,x)ds\frac{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})\Gamma(\frac{\beta}{2})}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2})}\int_{0}^{\infty}s^{\frac{\alpha+\beta}{2}-1}h(s,x)ds=P_{\alpha+\beta}(x),

and (
27
) is proved.

2.4.1.
Regularity

Here we deal with the regularity of the map
\alpha\to P_{\alpha}(x)
, for
x\in\mathbb{G}\setminus\{0\}
.

Proposition 2.15
.

For every
x\in\mathbb{G},\ x\neq 0
, the map

\displaystyle(-\infty,Q)\ni\alpha\to\|x\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)dt

is analytic.

Proof.

We fix
x\in\mathbb{G}
,
x\neq 0
,
t>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
and we compute the
k
-th derivative of the map
\alpha\to t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)
that is

\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial\alpha^{k}}\Big(t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)\Big)=t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x).

We notice that from the estimates in Item
1
of Section
2.4
we have

\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x)dt<\infty,

for every
\alpha<Q
, then we can deduce that for every
\alpha<Q
we have

\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial\alpha^{k}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)dt=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x)dt.

Now, we claim that for every closed interval
[a,b]\subset(-\infty,Q)
there are two positive constants
C
and
M
such that

(28)

\left|\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial\alpha^{k}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)dt\right|\leq CM^{k+1}k!,

for every
\alpha\in[a,b]
. This would conclude the proof. Hence, we need to prove (
28
). First, we consider

(29)

\left|\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial\alpha^{k}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)dt\right|\leq\left|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x)dt\right|+\left|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x)dt\right|.

By combining the homogeneity of
h
and Theorem
2.12
(vii), which ensures that
h(1,\cdot)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
, we can estimate the second term of the right-hand side of (
29
) getting

\displaystyle\left|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x)dt\right|

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x)dt

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(1,\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(x))dt

\displaystyle\leq\|h(1,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)dt.

Now, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
we set

(30)

\displaystyle a_{k}:

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha-Q}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)dt,

and we notice that

\displaystyle a_{k}

\displaystyle=\lim_{a\to\infty}\left[\frac{2}{\alpha-Q}t^{\frac{\alpha-Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)\right]_{t=1}^{a}+\int_{1}^{\infty}\frac{2}{Q-\alpha}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}k\ln^{k-1}(t)dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Q-\alpha}ka_{k-1},

where we have exploited the integration by parts.
Then, recursively we get that

(31)

a_{k}=k\frac{1}{Q-\alpha}a_{k-1}=k(k-1)\frac{1}{(Q-\alpha)^{2}}a_{k-2}=\ldots=k!\frac{1}{(Q-\alpha)^{k}}a_{0}=k!\frac{2}{(Q-\alpha)^{k+1}}.

Let us now focus on the first contribute of the right-hand side of (
29
).
We exploit the fact that
h(t,x)=o(t^{N})
as
t\to 0
for every
x\neq 0
and for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
(hence for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there is
c>0
such that
h(t,x)\leq ct^{N}
as
t<1
) and we can state that

\left|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x)dt\right|\leq c\left|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1+N}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)dt\right|\leq c\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1+N}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}dt.

Let us now set for
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
N\in\mathbb{N}
the term

(32)

\displaystyle b_{k}:

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1+N}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}dt.

We have that

\displaystyle b_{k}

\displaystyle=\lim_{a\to 0}\left[t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}+N}\frac{2}{\alpha+2N}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln^{k}(t)|\right]_{t=a}^{1}-\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\alpha+2N}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1+N}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k-1}k|\ln(t)|^{k-1}\mbox{\rm sgn}\,(\ln(t))dt.

If
N>-\frac{\alpha}{2}
, we can go on getting

(33)

\begin{split}b_{k}&=\int_{0}^{1}\frac{1}{\alpha+2N}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1+N}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k-1}k|\ln(t)|^{k-1}dt\\
&=\frac{1}{\alpha+2N}kb_{k-1}=\frac{1}{(\alpha+2N)^{2}}k(k-1)b_{k-2}=\ldots=\frac{1}{(\alpha+2N)^{k}}k!b_{0}=\frac{2}{(\alpha+2N)^{k+1}}k!.\\
\end{split}

By combining (
29
), (
31
) and (
33
), we get that

(34)

\begin{split}\left|\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial\alpha^{k}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)dt\right|\leq k!\|h(1,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\frac{2}{(Q-\alpha)^{k+1}}+\frac{2c}{(\alpha+2N)^{k+1}}k!\\
=2(1+c)(1+\|h(1,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})})k!\max\left\{\frac{1}{Q-\alpha},\frac{1}{\alpha+2N}\right\}^{k+1}.\end{split}

Since
N
can be chosen arbitrarily large (once fixed
\alpha
), our claim (
28
) is proved and this completes the proof.
∎

As a consequence of Proposition
2.15
, the map
\alpha\to P_{\alpha}(x)
is analytic for
\alpha<Q
, for every
x\in\mathbb{G},\ x\neq 0
, since it is a product of analytic functions.

Now we are in position to introduce the following map

(35)

(-\infty,Q)\ni\alpha\to\sigma(\alpha):=\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\|z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(z).

2.5.
Main properties of the map
\sigma(\alpha)

In this subsection we collect and prove some properties of the map
\sigma
defined in (
35
).

(1)

The map
\sigma(\alpha)
is well defined for every
\alpha<Q
. In fact, if we exploit the estimates in Item
1
of Section
2.4
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)dtd\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)dtd\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(x)+\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)dtd\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(x)

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}t^{N}d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(x)dt+c_{2}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}dtd\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1))\left(c_{1}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1+N}dt+c_{2}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}dt\right),

for some positive constants
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
for every arbitrarily fixed
N\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore,
\sigma(\alpha)
converges if
\alpha<Q
.

(2)

We show that
\alpha\to\sigma(\alpha)
is analytic if
\alpha<Q
. For every
k\in\mathbb{N}
we have

(36)

\begin{split}\left|\frac{\partial}{\partial\alpha^{k}}\sigma(\alpha)\right|&=\left|\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial\alpha^{k}}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)dtd\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(x)\right|\\
&=\left|\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial\alpha^{k}}\left(t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)\right)dtd\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(x)\right|\\
&=\left|\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x)dtd\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(x)\right|,\end{split}

since for every
\alpha<Q
and
k=0,1,\dots
we have that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x)dtd\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(x)\leq

(37)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1))\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x_{M})dt<\infty,

where
x_{M}\in\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)
is such that
h(t,x_{M})=\max_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x)dt\right)
. By combining (
36
), (
2
) and (
34
), we get that

\displaystyle\left|\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial\alpha^{k}}\sigma(\alpha)\right|

\displaystyle\leq\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1))\left|\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial\alpha^{k}}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x_{M})dt\right|

\displaystyle=\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1))2(1+c)(1+\|h(1,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})})k!\max\left\{\frac{1}{Q-\alpha},\frac{1}{\alpha+2N}\right\}^{k+1},

so that
\sigma(\alpha)
is analytic with respect to
\alpha
for
\alpha<Q
.

(3)

Let us prove that

\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\sigma(\alpha)=2,

hence that

\lim_{\alpha\to 0}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\|z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(z)=2.

Let us start by considering Theorem
2.12
(iii). By exploiting the coarea formula and the homogeneity of
\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}
, for every
t>0
we get

(38)

\begin{split}1=\int_{\mathbb{G}}h(t,x)dx&=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,r)}h(t,x)d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(x)dt\\
&=\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{Q-1}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}h(t,\delta_{r}(z))d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(z)dr.\end{split}

Now, let us consider

\displaystyle\sigma(\alpha)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\|z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,z)dtd\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(z)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}h(t,z)d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(z)dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}(\sqrt{t})^{-Q}h(1,\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(z))d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(z)dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}h(1,\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(z))d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(z)dt,

where we used the homogeneity of
h
. Let us change variable setting
r=\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}
, hence
t=r^{-2}
(
dt=-2r^{-3}
) and we get

\displaystyle\sigma(\alpha)

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\infty}^{0}r^{-\alpha+2+Q}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}h(1,\delta_{r}(z))d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(z)r^{-3}dr

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{-\alpha-1+Q}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}h(1,\delta_{r}(z))d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(z)dr,

which converges to

\displaystyle 2\int_{0}^{\infty}r^{Q-1}\int_{\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}h(1,\delta_{r}(z))d\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(z)dr,

as
\alpha\to 0
. Hence the proof of Item
3
is now complete taking into account (
38
).

3.
Analytic continuation in
\mathbb{G}

Let
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
and
x\in\mathbb{G}
.
For every
r>0
and
0<\alpha<Q
we denote the closed ball

\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}(x,r):=\{y\in\mathbb{G}:\|x^{-1}y\|_{\alpha}\leq r\}.

For every
\alpha>0
we introduce the map

(39)

\psi(x,\alpha)=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)P_{\alpha}(y)dy=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)\|y\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dy.

Proposition 3.1
.

For every
x\in\mathbb{G}
the map
(0,Q)\ni\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)
is well defined.

Proof.

By exploiting the estimate in (
25
), the fact that
\phi
is in the Schwartz class
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
on
\mathbb{G}
and the coarea formula of Corollary
2.8
, we have

(40)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)P_{\alpha}(y)dy&\leq\int_{\mathbb{G}}|\phi(xy)||P_{\alpha}(y)|dy=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{G}}|\phi(z)|\|x^{-1}z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dz\\
&=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}(x,1)}|\phi(z)|\|x^{-1}z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dz+\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}(x,1)}|\phi(z)|\|x^{-1}z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dz\\
&\leq\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(x,c_{\alpha})}\|x^{-1}z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dz+\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{\alpha}(x,1)}|\phi(z)|\frac{(1+\|z\|_{c}^{k})}{(1+\|z\|_{c}^{k})}dz,\end{split}

thus, taking into account the homogeneity of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}
and of the measure
\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}
, and the definition of
\sigma(\alpha)
we can continue from (
40
) getting

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)P_{\alpha}(y)dy

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\sigma(\alpha)\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\int_{0}^{c_{\alpha}}r^{\alpha-1}dr+\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}c_{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\frac{1}{(1+\|z\|_{c}^{k})}dz<\infty

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\frac{c_{\alpha}}{\alpha}\sigma(\alpha)\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}+\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}c_{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\frac{1}{(1+\|z\|_{c}^{k})}dz,

where, choosing
k>Q
, the last integral converges.
∎

The map
\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)
is well defined also if we weaken the hypothesis on
\phi
.

Proposition 3.2
.

If
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
and
|\phi(z)|\leq K\|z\|_{c}^{-L}
as
\|z\|_{c}\geq S
for some
L,K,S>0
then the map

(0,\min\{L,Q\})\ni\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)

is well defined.

Proof.

Keeping in mind that
x
is fixed, we consider
R(x):=\max\{1+\|x\|_{c},S\}
and we set
\widetilde{R}(x)>0
such that
\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))\subset\mathbb{B}_{c}(x,\widetilde{R}(x))
. For
\alpha>0
, by exploiting the hypotheses on
\phi
and the coarea formula in Corollary
2.8
, we get

(41)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)P_{\alpha}(y)dy&\leq\int_{\mathbb{G}}|\phi(xy)P_{\alpha}(y)|dy=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{G}}|\phi(z)|\|x^{-1}z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dz\\
=\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}&\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}|\phi(z)|\|x^{-1}z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dz+\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}|\phi(z)|\|x^{-1}z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dz\\
\leq\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}&\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\|x^{-1}z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dz+K\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\frac{1}{\|z\|_{c}^{L}}\|x^{-1}z\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dz.\end{split}

Thus, taking into account the homogeneity of the norm
\|\cdot\|_{\alpha}
and of the measure
\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}
, and the definition of
\sigma(\alpha)
, we can continue from (
40
) getting

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)P_{\alpha}(y)dy

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\sigma(\alpha)\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\int_{0}^{\widetilde{R}(x)}r^{\alpha-1}dr+K\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\frac{1}{\|z\|_{c}^{L+Q-\alpha}}\frac{\|z\|_{c}^{Q-\alpha}}{\|x^{-1}z\|_{\alpha}^{Q-\alpha}}dz

(42)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\sigma(\alpha)\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\int_{0}^{\widetilde{R}(x)}r^{\alpha-1}dr+K\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}(c_{\alpha}(\|x\|_{c}+1))^{Q-\alpha}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\frac{1}{\|z\|_{c}^{L+Q-\alpha}}dz,

since for every
z\notin\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))
, combining the definition of
R(x)
, the estimate (
25
) and some properties of homogeneous norms we have

\displaystyle\frac{\|x^{-1}z\|_{\alpha}}{\|z\|_{c}}\geq\frac{1}{c_{\alpha}}\frac{\|x^{-1}z\|_{c}}{\|z\|_{c}}=\frac{1}{c_{\alpha}}\|\delta_{\frac{1}{\|z\|_{c}}}(x)^{-1}\delta_{\frac{1}{\|z\|_{c}}}(z)\|_{c}

(43)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{c_{\alpha}}|\|\delta_{\frac{1}{\|z\|_{c}}}(x)\|_{c}-\|\delta_{\frac{1}{\|z\|_{c}}}(z)\|_{c}|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{c_{\alpha}}\bigg(1-\frac{\|x\|_{c}}{\|x\|_{c}+1}\bigg)=\frac{1}{c_{\alpha}}\frac{1}{\|x\|_{c}+1}.

Therefore, we can continue from (
3
) getting

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)P_{\alpha}(y)dy\leq\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\sigma(\alpha)\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\int_{0}^{\widetilde{R}(x)}r^{\alpha-1}dr

\displaystyle+K\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}(c_{\alpha}(\|x\|_{c}+1))^{Q-\alpha}\mathcal{S}^{Q-1}_{\infty}(\partial\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1))\int_{R(x)}^{\infty}r^{\alpha-L-1}dr.

The last term of previous equation converges for every
\alpha>0
such that
L+1-\alpha>1
, hence when
\alpha<L
. This concludes the proof.
∎

From now on, in the current section we show that the map
\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)
is analytic for
0<\alpha<Q
and we construct an analytic continuation of
\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)
on
-2<\alpha\leq 0
. This construction allows to introduce a consistent suitable definition of negative powers of the sub-Laplacian
\mathcal{L}
. We mainly follow the structure of
[
27
, Chapter 1]
, where an analogous construction is carried out in Euclidean spaces.

3.1.
The map
\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)
is analytic on
(0,Q)

In this subsection, we show that the map
\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)
is analytic on
(0,Q)
. First, we consider the following remark.

Remark 3.3
.

For every
x\neq 0
, we know that
h(t,x)=o(t^{N})
for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
. This property is uniform for
x\in\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}(0,r)
, for every
r>0
. In fact, by Theorem
2.12
(vi), there exists a constant
c>0
such that for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
and
t>0

c^{-1}t^{-Q/2}\mbox{\rm exp}\;\!\left(-\frac{\|x\|^{2}}{c^{-1}t}\right)\leq h(t,x)\leq ct^{-Q/2}\mbox{\rm exp}\;\!\left(-\frac{\|x\|^{2}}{ct}\right),

hence for every
y\in\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,r)
and
t>0
the estimate

h(t,y)\leq ct^{-Q/2}e^{-\frac{r}{ct}}

holds, so that for every
N\in\mathbb{N}

t^{-N}h(t,y)\leq ct^{-Q/2-N}e^{-\frac{r}{ct}}\to 0

as
t\to 0
independently of
y
. We are ready to treat the regularity of
\psi(x,\alpha)
.

Proposition 3.4
.

For every
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
and
x\in\mathbb{G}
the function

\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)P_{\alpha}(y)dy

is analytic on
(0,Q).

Proof.

Since the map
\alpha\to\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}
is analytic on
\mathbb{R}
and analytic functions well behave with respect to the product, it is enough to prove that

\alpha\to\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)\|y\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dy=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,y)dtdy

is analytic on
(0,Q).
Let us consider that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
for every
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{G}

\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial\alpha^{k}}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,x)=t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,x).

We obtain the needed estimate on the
k
-th derivative of the map
\psi(x,\alpha)
, showing at the same time that it is summable on
(0,\infty)\times\mathbb{G}

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,y)dtdy\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{G}}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

(44)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy.

Let us split the second term of (
3.1
) as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

(45)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,y)dtdy.

Let us then consider the first term of the sum in (
3.1
), getting the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dy

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(x\cdot)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{B}(0,1)}h(t,y)dydt

(46)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(x\cdot)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}}h(t,y)dydt

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(x\cdot)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}dt.

Now, consider that
\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}dt
equals the
b_{k}
defined in (
32
) for
N=0>-\frac{\alpha}{2}
, hence we can continue (
3.1
) exploiting the estimate in (
33
) and we get that

(47)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dy\leq\sup_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(x\cdot)|\ \frac{2}{\alpha^{k+1}}k!.\end{split}

Let us bound the second term of the sum in (
3.1
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,y)dtdy

(48)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(1,\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(y))dtdy

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(x\cdot)|\sup_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}h(1,\cdot)\mu(\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1))\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)dt.

Now, consider that
\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)dt
equals the
a_{k}
introduced in (
30
), hence we can continue from (
3.1
) exploiting the estimate in (
31
) and we get that

(49)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,y)dtdy\\
&\leq\sup_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(x\cdot)|\sup_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}h(1,\cdot)\mu(\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1))\frac{2}{(Q-\alpha)^{k+1}}k!.\end{split}

Let us split the first term of the sum in (
3.1
) as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

(50)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,y)dtdy.

Keeping in mind Remark
3.3
, we can estimate the first term of the sum in (
3.1
) as follows for
N\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

\displaystyle\leq\

\displaystyle c\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1+N}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|dydt

(51)

\displaystyle\leq\

\displaystyle c\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1+N}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}}|\phi(z)|\frac{(1+\|z\|_{c}^{\ell})}{(1+\|z\|_{c}^{\ell})}dzdt

\displaystyle\leq\

\displaystyle cc_{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{G}}\frac{1}{1+\|z\|_{c}^{\ell}}dz\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1+N}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}dt

\displaystyle\leq\

\displaystyle c\widetilde{c}_{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1+N}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}dt,

where
c
,
c_{\ell}
and
\widetilde{c}_{\ell}
are suitable positive constants and
\ell
is understood such that
\ell>Q
.
Analogously to (
3.1
), when
N>-\frac{\alpha}{2}
we can bound (
3.1
) as follows

(52)

\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1+N}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy\leq\tilde{c}_{\ell}\frac{2}{(\alpha+2N)^{k+1}}k!.\end{split}

Let us finally consider the second term of the sum in (
3.1
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln(t)^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln(t)^{k}h(1,\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(y))dtdy

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln(t)^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|h(1,\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(y))dydt

(53)

\displaystyle\leq\|h(1,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)dt\int_{\mathbb{G}}|\phi(h)|dh

\displaystyle=\|h(1,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)dt\int_{\mathbb{G}}|\phi(h)|\frac{(1+\|h\|_{c}^{\ell})}{(1+\|h\|_{c}^{\ell})}dh

\displaystyle\leq c_{\ell}\|h(1,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)\int_{\mathbb{G}}\frac{1}{(1+\|h\|_{c}^{\ell})}dhdt

\displaystyle\leq\widetilde{c}_{\ell}\|h(1,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)dt,

where
c_{\ell}
and
\widetilde{c}_{\ell}
are suitable positive constants depending on
\ell
, and
\ell
is understood to be strictly larger than
Q
. Now, analogously to the reasoning used in (
3.1
), exploiting the estimate in (
31
), we get

(54)

\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy\leq c_{\ell}\|h(1,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\frac{2}{(Q-\alpha)^{k+1}}k!.

Now, by combining (
3.1
), (
3.1
), (
3.1
), (
47
), (
49
), (
52
) and (
54
), we get that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,y)dtdy\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbb{G}}|\phi(x\cdot)|\ \frac{2}{\alpha^{k+1}}k!+\sup_{\mathbb{G}}|\phi(x\cdot)|\sup_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}h(1,\cdot)\mu(\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1))\frac{2}{(Q-\alpha)^{k+1}}k!

(55)

\displaystyle+\widetilde{c}_{\ell}\frac{2}{(\alpha+2N)^{k+1}}k!+\widetilde{c}_{\ell}\|h(1,\cdot)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})}\frac{2}{(Q-\alpha)^{k+1}}k!

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C(\phi,\ell)\max\left\{\frac{1}{\alpha+2N},\frac{1}{Q-\alpha},\frac{1}{\alpha}\right\}^{k+1}k!,

where
c(\phi,\ell)
is a suitable positive constant.
Thus,
\psi(x,\alpha)
is analytic on
0<\alpha<Q
with respect to
\alpha
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Following the scheme of the previous proof, exploiting the estimates in (
52
) and (
54
), it holds that the map

\alpha\to\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\phi(xy)\|y\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dt

is analytic for
\alpha<Q
(where it surely converges by direct computations similar to the ones in Proposition
3.1
).

3.2.
Further properties of the map
\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)

Here we generalize Proposition
3.4
. We assume that
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
and
|\phi(z)|\leq K\|z\|_{c}^{-L}
when
\|z\|_{c}\geq S
for some
L,S,K>0
.

Proposition 3.6
.

For any
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{G})
such that
|\phi(z)|\leq K\|z\|_{c}^{-L}
when
\|z\|_{c}\geq S
for some
L,S,K>0
, for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
the map

\alpha\to\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)P_{\alpha}(y)dy

is analytic on
(0,\min\{L,Q\})
.

Proof.

It is enough to prove that

\alpha\to\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)\|y\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dy=\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}h(t,y)dtdy

is analytic on
(0,\min\{L,Q\})
.
Let
x\in\mathbb{G}
and set
R(x)=\max\{1+\|x\|_{c},S\}
. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
we consider

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,y)dtdy\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

(56)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy.

By computations analogous to the ones used in the previous Proposition
3.4
, one verifies that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

(57)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(x\cdot)|\sup_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|h(1,\cdot)|2\max\left\{\frac{1}{\alpha},\frac{1}{Q-\alpha}\right\}^{k+1}k!.

Let us split the first term of the sum in (
3.2
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

(58)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy.

Let us consider the first term of the sum in (
3.2
) as follows

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy

(59)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(1,\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(y))dtdy

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|h(1,\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(y))dydt.

Let us change the variables as
z=\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(y)
, so that, since the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{G}
is
Q
-homogeneous, we get the estimate

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|h(1,\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(y))dydt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x)))}|\phi(x\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(z))|h(1,z)dz\ t^{\frac{Q}{2}}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x)))}|\phi(x\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(z))|h(1,z)dzdt

\displaystyle\leq K\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x)))}\frac{1}{\|x\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(z)\|_{c}^{L}}h(1,z)\frac{\|z\|_{c}^{\ell}}{\|z\|_{c}^{\ell}}dzdt

\displaystyle\leq Kc_{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x)))}\frac{1}{\|x\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(z)\|_{c}^{L}}\frac{1}{\|z\|_{c}^{\ell}}dzdt,

where
c_{\ell}
is a suitable positive constant obtained exploiting the fact that the map
h(1,\cdot)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
, for an arbitrary
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
. Let us change again variables, setting
h=x\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(z)
and considering that the Lebesgue measure is left-invariant and
Q
-homogeneous, so that we get the estimate

\displaystyle Kc_{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x)))}\frac{1}{\|x\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(z)\|_{c}^{L}}\frac{1}{\|z\|_{c}^{\ell}}dzdt

\displaystyle\leq Kc_{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\frac{1}{\|h\|_{c}^{L}}\frac{1}{\|\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(x^{-1}h)\|_{c}^{\ell}}dhdt

(60)

\displaystyle\leq Kc_{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\frac{1}{\|h\|_{c}^{L}}\frac{t^{\frac{\ell}{2}}}{\|x^{-1}h\|_{c}^{\ell}}dhdt

\displaystyle\leq Kc_{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}+\frac{\ell}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\frac{1}{\|h\|_{c}^{L}}\frac{\|h\|^{\ell}}{\|x^{-1}h\|_{c}^{\ell}}\frac{1}{\|h\|_{c}^{\ell}}dhdt

\displaystyle\leq Kc_{\ell}\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}+\frac{\ell}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}(1+\|x\|_{c})^{\ell}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\frac{1}{\|h\|_{c}^{L+\ell}}dhdt.

We have used an argument analogous to the one used in (
3
).
The integral in the last line of (
3.2
) converges for every
\ell>Q-L
. If we choose some
\ell>Q-L
large enough that also
\ell-Q>-\alpha
holds, then we can choose also
\ell>Q
and we can introduce
N=[\frac{\ell-Q}{2}]+1
and we can repeat the computations leading to (
33
) getting

(61)

\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},1)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{0}^{1}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}h(t,y)dtdy\leq K\widetilde{c}_{\ell}\frac{2}{(\alpha+(\ell-Q))^{k+1}}k!.

Let us then consider the second term of the sum in (
3.2
)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,y)dtdy

(62)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(1,\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(y))dtdy

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|h(1,\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(y))dydt.

Let us change the variables as
z=\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(y)
, so that, since the Lebesgue measure is
Q
-homogeneous we get

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}|\phi(xy)|h(1,\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(y))dydt

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x)))}|\phi(x\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(z))|h(1,z)dz\ t^{\frac{Q}{2}}dt

\displaystyle=\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x)))}|\phi(x\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(z))|h(1,z)dzdt

\displaystyle\leq K\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x)))}\frac{1}{\|x\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(z)\|_{c}^{L}}h(1,z)\frac{\|z\|_{c}^{\ell}}{\|z\|^{\ell}}dzdt

\displaystyle\leq Kc_{\ell}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x)))}\frac{1}{\|x\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(z)\|_{c}^{L}}\frac{1}{\|z\|^{\ell}}dzdt,

where
c_{\ell}
denotes a suitable positive constant depending on
\ell
, obtained considering that
h(1,\cdot)\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
.
Let us change again variables, setting
h=x\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(z)
and recalling that the Lebesgue measure on
\mathbb{G}
is left-invariant and
Q
-homogeneous, so that we get

\displaystyle Kc_{\ell}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}|\ln(t)|^{k}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x)))}\frac{1}{\|x\delta_{\sqrt{t}}(z)\|_{c}^{L}}\frac{1}{\|z\|^{\ell}}dzdt

\displaystyle\leq Kc_{\ell}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\frac{1}{\|h\|_{c}^{L}}\frac{1}{\|\delta_{\frac{1}{\sqrt{t}}}(x^{-1}h)\|^{\ell}}dzdt

\displaystyle\leq Kc_{\ell}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\frac{1}{\|h\|_{c}^{L}}\frac{t^{\frac{\ell}{2}}}{\|x^{-1}h\|^{\ell}}dzdt

\displaystyle\leq Kc_{\ell}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}+\frac{\ell}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)dt\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\frac{1}{\|h\|_{c}^{L}}\frac{\|h\|^{\ell}}{\|x^{-1}h\|^{\ell}}\frac{1}{\|h\|^{\ell}}dz

\displaystyle\leq Kc_{\ell}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{Q}{2}+\frac{\ell}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)(1+\|x\|_{c})^{\ell}dt\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\frac{1}{\|h\|_{c}^{L+\ell}}dz

\displaystyle\leq Kc_{\ell}\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1-\frac{L}{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)(1+\|x\|_{c})^{\ell}dt\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,R(x))}\frac{1}{\|h\|_{c}^{L+\ell}}dz.

The integral in the last line is justified if we consider
\ell>Q-L
, hence when
L>Q-\ell
. Now, similarly to the procedure applied to deal with (
3.1
), exploiting an estimate analogous to (
31
), we can conclude that

(63)

\begin{split}\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R)}|\phi(xy)|\int_{1}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,y)dtdy&\leq Kc_{\ell}\frac{2}{(L-\alpha)^{k+1}}k!.\end{split}

Now, by combining (
3.2
), (
3.2
), (
61
) and (
63
), we get that for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and for every
\ell>\max\{Q-L,Q-\alpha,2Q\}

(64)

\begin{split}&\left|\int_{\mathbb{G}}\phi(xy)\int_{0}^{\infty}t^{\frac{\alpha}{2}-1}\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^{k}\ln^{k}(t)h(t,y)dtdy\right|\\
&\leq C\max\left\{\frac{1}{Q-\alpha},\frac{1}{L-\alpha},\frac{1}{\alpha},\frac{1}{\alpha+(\ell-Q)}\right\}^{k+1}k!,\end{split}

for some suitable constant
C>0
. Therefore,
\psi(x,\alpha)
is analytic on
0<\alpha<\min\{Q,L\}
.
∎

Remark 3.7
.

Following the previous proof, in the same hypotheses of Proposition
3.6
, we obtain that the map

\alpha\to\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R(x))}\phi(xy)\|y\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dt

is analytic on
(-\infty,\min\{Q,L\})
. It is enough to consider (
3.2
) and the estimates in (
61
) and (
63
) that are valid for every
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and for every
\ell>\max\{Q-L,Q-\alpha,2Q\}
. The analyticity is guaranteed on
(-\infty,\min\{Q,L\})
also for the map

\alpha\to\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\phi(xy)\|y\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dt,

since, in order to estimate the
k
-th derivative of

\alpha\to\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(x^{-1},R)\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\phi(xy)\|y\|_{\alpha}^{\alpha-Q}dt,

one can simplify the arguments carried out in the proof of Proposition
3.4
.

3.3.
The map
\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)
is analytically continued on (-2,0]

In this subsection, we build up the analytic continuation of
\alpha\to\psi(x,\alpha)
on the strip
(-2,0]
.

Theorem 3.8
.

For every
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
and
x\in\mathbb{G}
, the map

(0,Q)\to\psi(x,\alpha)

can be analytically continued to the interval
(-2,Q)
. Moreover, the representation

(65)

\displaystyle\psi(x,\alpha)=\int_{\mathbb{G}\setminus\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}\phi(xy)P_{\alpha}(y)dy+\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}(\phi(xy)-\phi(x))P_{\alpha}(y)dy+\phi(x)\frac{1}{\Gamma(\frac{\alpha}{2})}\frac{1}{\alpha}\sigma(\alpha)

holds for
\alpha\in(-2,Q)
.

Before proving Theorem
3.8
, we prove two technical results concerning the convergence and the regularity of the second term of the sum in (
65
), respectively.

Lemma 3.9
.

For every
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{G})
and for every
x\in\mathbb{G}
the integral

\int_{\mathbb{B}_{c}(0,1)}(\phi(xy)-\phi(x))P_{\alpha}(y)dy

converges for every
-2<\alpha<Q
.

Proof.

By the coarea formula of Corollary
2.8
